2.8. Formal open immersions and formal open coverings. Let us say that
a homomorphism of (discrete) commutative rings f : R −→S is an open immer-
sion if the induced map of the spectra Spec S −→Spec R is an open immersion of
affine schemes. Equivalently, f is an open immersion if and only if it is a flat ring
epimorphism of finite presentation [40, Section XI.2], [11, Theoreme IV.17.9.1]. In
particular, we will say that a homomorphism of commutative rings R −→S is a
principal open immersion if there exists an element r ∈R such that S is isomorphic
to R[r−1] as a commutative R-algebra.
Similarly, let us say that a collection of homomorphisms of commutative rings
fα : R −→Sα is a (principal) open covering if every map fα is a (principal) open
immersion and the collection of the induced maps of the spectra Spec Sα −→Spec R is
an affine open covering of the affine scheme Spec R. Equivalently, the latter condition
means that, for any field k and any ring homomorphism R −→k, there exists an
index α such that Sα ⊗R k ̸= 0. The following criterion is a basic result concerning
affine open subschemes in affine schemes.
Lemma 2.8.1. A homomorphism of commutative rings f : R −→S is an open
immersion if and only if there exists a finite collection of elements r1, ..., rm ∈R
such that the induced ring maps R[r−1
j ] −→S[f(rj)−1] are isomorphisms for all 1 ≤
j ≤m and the collection of principal open immersions S −→S[f(rj)−1], 1 ≤j ≤m,
is an open covering.
□
Lemma 2.8.2. Let f : R −→S be a homomorphism of commutative rings such that
S is a flat R-module, and let I ⊂R be a nilpotent ideal. Then
(a) f : R −→S is an isomorphism if and only if ¯f : R/I −→S/Sf(I) is an
isomorphism;
30


## Page 31


(b) f : R −→S is a principal open immersion if and only if ¯f : R/I −→S/Sf(I)
is a principal open immersion;
(c) f : R −→S is an open immersion if and only if ¯f : R/I −→S/Sf(I) is an
open immersion.
Proof. Part (a): more generally, for any nilpotent ideal I in a commutative ring R,
a homomorphism of flat R-modules f : F −→G is an isomorphism if and only if
the induced map ¯f : F/IF −→G/IG is an isomorphism. This is provable using the
Nakayama lemma for nilpotent ideals.
Part (b): the “only if” assertion is obvious. To prove the “if”, let ¯r ∈R/I be an
element such that the R/I-algebra S/Sf(I) is isomorphic to (R/I)[¯r−1]. Let r ∈R
be any element such that ¯r = r + I. Applying part (a) to the R-algebra morphisms
R[r−1] −→S[f(r)−1] ←−S and the nilpotent ideals I[r−1] ⊂R[r−1] and SI ⊂S,
one concludes that both of these R-algebra morphisms are isomorphisms.
Part (c): once again, the “only if” assertion is obvious. To prove the “if”, let ¯r1, . . . ,
¯rm ∈R/I be a finite collection of elements satisfying the conditions of Lemma 2.8.1 for
the ring homomorphism R/I −→S/Sf(I). Let rj ∈R, 1 ≤j ≤m, be any element
such that ¯rj = rj + I. Applying part (a) to the ring homomorphisms R[r−1
j ] −→
S[f(rj)−1] and the nilpotent ideals I[r−1
j ] ⊂R[r−1
j ], one concludes that these ring
homomorphisms are isomorphisms. To prove that the collection of principal open
immersions S −→S[f(rj)−1], 1 ≤j ≤m, is an open covering, it suffices to observe
that any homomorphism from S to a field k factorizes through S/Sf(I).
□
Lemma 2.8.3. Let fα : R −→Sα be a collection of homomorphisms of commuta-
tive rings such that the rings Sα are flat R-modules, and let I ⊂R be a nilpotent
ideal. Consider the induced ring homomorphisms ¯fα : R/I −→Sα/Sαfα(I). Then
the collection (fα) is an open covering if and only if the collection ( ¯fα) is an open
covering.
Proof. One needs to use Lemma 2.8.2(c). On top of that, the final argument from
the proof of the same lemma works: Any ring homomorphism from R to a field k
factorizes through R/I.
□
Lemma 2.8.4. A homomorphism of commutative rings f : R −→S is an open
immersion if and only if there exists an open covering gα : S −→Tα of the ring S
such that the compositions gαf : R −→Tα are open immersions. If this is the case,
then one can choose (gα) to be a principal open covering for which all the compositions
gαf are also principal open immersions.
Proof. The first assertion expresses locality of the notion of an open immersion of
schemes. It is immediate from the definitions. The second assertion is a restatement
of the fact that principal affine open subschemes form a topology base in Spec R.
□
Theorem 2.8.5. Let R be a commutative ring and I ⊂R be a nilpotent ideal.
Then the functor S 7−→S/Sf(I) provides an equivalence between the category of
commutative R-algebras f : R −→S such that f is an open immersion and the cate-
gory of commutative R/I-algebras ¯f : R/I −→S such that ¯f is an open immersion.
31


## Page 32


In other words, the map Spec S 7−→Spec S/Sf(I) is an isomorphism between the
poset of affine open subschemes in Spec R and the poset of affine open subschemes
in Spec R/I (with respect to the inclusion order). An open immersion f is principal
if and only if the open immersion ¯f is.
Proof. We are not aware of any argument within the realm of commutative ring
theory. The following proof is, basically, a collection of references. By [11, The-
orem IV.18.1.2], the functor X 7−→Spec R/I ×Spec R X is an equivalence between
the category of ´etale schemes over Spec R and the category of ´etale schemes over
Spec R/I. By [11, Theorem IV.17.9.1], an ´etale morphism is an open immersion if
and only if it is radicial (in the sense of [12, Section I.3.7]). It follows easily that
an ´etale morphism X −→Spec R is an open immersion if and only if the corre-
sponding morphism Spec R/I ×Spec R X −→Spec R/I is an open immersion. So the
map Spec R/I ×Spec R −is an equivalence between the category of open subschemes
in Spec R and the category of open subschemes in Spec R/I. The same conclusion
can be simply obtained from the facts that open subschemes in schemes correspond
bijectively to open subsets in the underlying topology, and the underlying topological
spaces of Spec R and Spec R/I coincide.
The key step is to prove that an open subscheme Y ⊂Spec R is affine if and only if
the scheme Spec R/I ×Spec R Y is affine. The “only if” assertion is obvious. To prove
the “if”, notice that the underlying topological spaces of Y and Spec R/I ×Spec R Y
coincide. Quasi-compactness and quasi-separatedness are properties of the under-
lying topological space of a scheme; and all affine schemes are quasi-compact and
quasi-separated. So the scheme Y is quasi-compact and quasi-separated (in fact, sep-
arated, as an open subscheme of an affine scheme). By [9, Th´eor`eme II.5.2.1(d)], [10,
IV.1.7.17], [14, Lemmas Tags 01XB and 01XF or 01XG], a quasi-compact scheme Y
is affine if and only if H1(Y, F) = 0 for every quasi-coherent sheaf F on Y . Here
H∗(Y, −) denotes the cohomology groups of sheaves of abelian groups on Y , which
only depend on the underlying topological space of Y . It remains to point out that
every quasi-coherent sheaf F on Y has a finite decreasing filtration by quasi-coherent
subsheaves F ⊃IF ⊃I2F ⊃· · · ⊃InF = 0, where n ≥1 is an integer such that
In = 0 in R, and the successive quotient sheaves ImF/Im+1F are quasi-coherent
sheaves on Spec R/I ×Spec R Y .
Finally, if f : R −→S is an open immersion and the R/I-algebra S/Sf(I) is
isomorphic to (R/I)[¯r−1] for some element ¯r ∈R/I, then the R-algebra S is isomor-
phic to R[r−1] for any element r ∈R such that ¯r = r + I. This assertion follows
immediately from the bijection estalished above.
□
Lemma 2.8.6. Let f : R −→S be a tight continuous map of adic topological rings.
Then the following three conditions are equivalent:
(1) for every ideal of definition I ⊂R, the induced ring map ¯f : R/I −→S/Sf(I)
is an open immersion;
(2) there exists an ideal of definition I ⊂R such that the induced ring map
¯f : R/In −→S/Sf(In) is an open immersion for every n ≥1;
32


## Page 33


(3) f is flat (as a map of adic topological rings) and there exists an ideal of
definition I ⊂R such that the induced ring map ¯f : R/I −→S/Sf(I) is an
open immersion.
Proof. (1) =⇒(2) Holds because In ⊂R is an ideal of definition for any ideal of
definition I ⊂R.
(2) =⇒(1) The point is that if I ⊂I′ ⊂R are two ideals and the ring map
R/I −→S/Sf(I) is an open immersion, then the ring map R/I′ −→S/Sf(I′) is an
open immersion, too.
(1) =⇒(3) Holds because, for all open immersions of commutative rings ¯f : R −→
S, the ring S is a flat R-module.
(3) =⇒(2) Follows from Lemma 2.8.2(c).
□
We will say that a tight continuous map of adic topological rings f : R −→S is a
formal open immersion if it satisfies the equivalent conditions of Lemma 2.8.6. Using
the criterion of Lemma 2.8.6(1), one can easily check that the composition of any
two formal open immersions is a formal open immersion.
Lemma 2.8.7. Let f : R −→S be a continuous map of adic topological rings. Con-
sider the ring S ⊗R S, and endow it with the tensor product topology. In this context:
(a) the induced map of topological rings S ⊗R S −→S is continuous and tight;
(b) the induced maps of topological rings S ⇒S ⊗R S are continuous;
(c) if the map f is tight, then so are the maps S ⇒S ⊗R S;
(d) if the map f is a formal open immersion, then all the three induced maps of
the completions
Λ(S) ⇒Λ(S ⊗R S) −→Λ(S)
are isomorphisms of topological rings.
Proof. Parts (a) and (b) do not depend on the topology on R (one can use the
discrete topology on R, for example).
Part (a): denote the map in question by
p: S ⊗R S −→S. Let J ⊂S be an ideal of definition. Then, by Lemma 2.7.1(d),
J⊗RS+S⊗RJ is an ideal of definition in S⊗RS. Now we have J = p(J⊗RS+S⊗RJ),
so p is a tight continuous map by Lemma 2.1.6(4).
Part (b) follows from the discussion in Section 2.7 (including Lemma 2.7.2(a)).
Part (c): denote the maps in question by i1 and i2 : S ⇒S ⊗R S. Let I ⊂R
be an ideal of definition. By Lemma 2.1.6(5), the ideal J = Sf(I) ⊂S is an ideal
of definition in S. By Lemma 2.7.1(d), the ideal J ⊗R S + S ⊗R J ⊂S ⊗R S is
an ideal of definition in S ⊗R S. Now we have Sf(I) ⊗R S = S ⊗R Sf(I), hence
J ⊗R S + S ⊗R J = J ⊗R S = S ⊗R J ⊂S ⊗R S. By Lemma 2.1.6(4), it follows that
i1 and i2 are tight ring maps.
Part (d): in the notation of the previous paragraph, let I range over all the ideals
of definition in R.
Then the ideals J = Sf(I) form a base of neighborhoods of
zero in S, while the ideals J ⊗R S = S ⊗R J form a base of neighborhoods of zero
in S ⊗R S.
Now we have S/(J ⊗R S + S ⊗R J) ≃S/J ⊗R/I S/J.
The natural
maps S/J ⇒S/J ⊗R/I S/J −→S/J are ring isomorphisms, since R/I −→S/J
33


## Page 34


is an open immersion (of abstract commutative rings) by Lemma 2.8.6(1). Passing
to the projective limit over I, we obtain the desired isomorphisms of complete adic
topological rings.
□
Lemma 2.8.8. Let f : R −→S be a tight continuous map of adic topological rings.
Then the following four conditions are equivalent:
(1) for every ideal of definition I ⊂R, the induced ring map ¯f : R/I −→S/Sf(I)
is a principal open immersion;
(2) there exists an ideal of definition I ⊂R such that the induced ring map
¯f : R/In −→S/Sf(In) is a principal open immersion for every n ≥1;
(3) f is flat (as a map of adic topological rings) and there exists an ideal of
definition I ⊂R such that the induced ring map ¯f : R/I −→S/Sf(I) is a
principal open immersion;
(4) there exists an element r ∈R such that, endowing the ring R[r−1] with the adic
topology for which the natural ring map l: R −→R[r−1] is continuous and
tight, there exists a unique isomorphism of topological rings Λ(S) ≃Λ(R[r−1])
making the following diagram commutative:

Proof. (1) =⇒(2) and (1) =⇒(3) Similar to the proof of Lemma 2.8.6.
(2) =⇒(1) The point is that if I ⊂I′ ⊂R are two ideals and the ring map R/I −→
S/Sf(I) is a principal open immersion, then the ring map R/I′ −→S/Sf(I′) is a
principal open immersion, too.
(3) =⇒(2) Follows from Lemma 2.8.2(b).
(4) =⇒(1) Obvious.
(4) =⇒(3) Follows from Lemma 2.3.1(b).
(3) =⇒(4) Let ¯r ∈R/I be an element such that the R/I-algebra S/Sf(I) is
isomorphic to (R/I)[¯r−1]. Pick any element r ∈R for which ¯r = r + I. Let n ≥1 be
an integer; put ˜r = r+In ∈R/In. By the proof of Lemma 2.8.2(b), the R/In-algebra
S/Sf(In) is isomorphic to (R/In)[˜r−1]. As the category of open subschemes in Spec R
is a poset, an isomorphism of R/In-algebras (R/In)[˜r−1] ≃S/Sf(In) is unique.
Hence, as n varies, such isomorphisms form a projective system.
Passing to the
projective limit over n ≥1, we obtain the desired isomorphism of adic topological
rings and Λ(R)-algebras Λ(R[r−1]) ≃Λ(S).
□
We will say that a tight continuous map of adic topological rings f : R −→
S is a principal formal open immersion if it satisfies the equivalent conditions of
Lemma 2.8.8. Using the criterion of Lemma 2.8.8(1), one can easily check that the
composition of any two principal formal open immersions is a principal formal open
immersion.
34


## Page 35


Corollary 2.8.9. Let R be an adic topological ring and I ⊂R be an ideal of defini-
tion. Then the functor S 7−→S/Sf(I) provides an equivalence between the category
of commutative R-algebras f : R −→S with an adic topology on S such that f is a
formal open immersion and the category of commutative R/I-algebras ¯f : R/I −→S
such that ¯f is an open immersion. A formal open immersion f is principal if and
only if the open immersion ¯f is principal.
Proof. This is a corollary of Theorem 2.8.5. Let us just explain how to construct
the inverse functor. Suppose given an open immersion ¯f : R/I −→S. By Theo-
rem 2.8.5, for every n ≥1 there exists an open immersion fn : R/In −→Sn to-
gether with a R/I-algebra isomorphism R/I ⊗R/In Sn ≃S. The uniqueness assertion
of Theorem 2.8.5 implies existence and uniqueness of R/Im-algebra isomorphisms
R/Im ⊗R/In Sn ≃Sm for all n ≥m ≥1. It remains to set S = lim
←−n≥1 Sn. The result
of [42, Theorem 1.2 or 2.8] or [18, Lemma E.1.3] is helpful here. To prove the final
assertion of the corollary, one needs to use Lemma 2.8.8(1).
□
Lemma 2.8.10. Let R, S, and T be adic topological rings, let f : R −→S be a
tight continuous ring map, and let g: R −→T be a continuous ring map.
Put
W = T ⊗R S, and denote by f ′ : T −→W and g′ : S −→W the induced ring maps.
Endow the commutative ring W with the tensor product topology, as in Section 2.7.
In this setting:
(a) if the tight continuous ring map f is a formal open immersion, then the tight
continuous ring map f ′ is a formal open immersion, too;
(b) if the tight continuous ring map f is a principal formal open immersion, then
the tight continuous ring map f ′ is a principal formal open immersion, too.
Proof. Similar to the proof of Lemma 2.7.3(b).
□
Lemma 2.8.11. Let fα : R −→Sα be a collection of tight continuous maps of adic
topological rings. Then the following three conditions are equivalent:
(1) for every ideal of definition I ⊂R, the collection of induced ring maps
¯fα : R/I −→Sα/Sαfα(I) is an open covering;
(2) there exists an ideal of definition I ⊂R such that the collection of induced
ring maps ¯fα : R/In −→Sα/Sαfα(In) is an open covering for every n ≥1;
(3) the maps fα are flat (as maps of adic topological rings) and there exists an ideal
of definition I ⊂R such that the collection of induced ring maps ¯fα : R/I −→
Sα/Sαfα(I) is an open covering.
Proof. Follows from Lemmas 2.8.3 and 2.8.6.
□
We will say that a collection of tight continuous maps of adic topological rings
fα : R −→Sα is a formal open covering if it satisfies the equivalent conditions of
Lemma 2.8.11. It is clear from Lemma 2.8.11(3) that any formal open covering of
an adic topological ring has a finite subcovering. A principal formal open covering
is a formal open covering (fα) such that every map fα is a principal formal open
immersion.
35


## Page 36


Lemma 2.8.12. A tight continuous map of adic topological rings f : R −→S is an
formal open immersion if and only if there exists a formal open covering gα : S −→Tα
of the adic topological ring S such that the compositions gαf : R −→Tα are formal
open immersions.
If this is the case, then one can choose (gα) to be a principal
formal open covering for which all the compositions gαf are also principal formal
open immersions.
Proof. First assertion: to prove the “only if”, it suffices to take the set of indices
{α} to be the singleton {0}, and the trivial formal open covering with T0 = S and
g0 = idS. To prove the “if”, let I ⊂R be an ideal of definition. By Lemma 2.1.6(5),
the ideal Sf(I) ⊂S is an ideal of definition in S, while the ideals Tαgαf(I) ⊂Tα
are ideals of definition in Tα. By Lemma 2.8.11(1), the collection of induced ring
maps ¯gα : S/Sf(I) −→Tα/Tαgαf(I) is an open covering. By Lemma 2.8.6(1), the
compositions ¯gα ¯f : R/I −→Tα/Tαgαf(I) are open immersions. By the first assertion
of Lemma 2.8.4, it follows that the induced ring map ¯f : R/I −→S/Sf(I) is an open
immersion. Using Lemma 2.8.6(1) again, we conclude that f : R −→S is a formal
open immersion.
Second assertion: let I ⊂R be an ideal of definition. By Lemma 2.8.6(1), the
induced ring map ¯f : R/I −→S/Sf(I) is an open immersion. By the sectond as-
sertion of Lemma 2.8.4, there exists a principal open covering ¯gα : S/Sf(I) −→T α
such that the compositions ¯gα ¯f : R/I −→T α are principal open immersions. By
Corollary 2.8.9, there exist principal formal open immersions gα : S −→Tα together
with S/Sf(I)-algebra isomorphisms Tα/Tαgαf(I) ≃T α. By Lemma 2.8.11(1), the
collection (gα) is a formal open covering.
□
Lemma 2.8.13. Let R and S be adic topological rings, let f : R −→S be a continuous
ring map, and let gα : R −→Tα be a formal open covering. Put Wα = Tα ⊗R S, and
denote by f ′
α : Tα −→Wα and g′
α : S −→Wα the induced ring maps. Endow the
commutative rings Wα with the tensor product topologies. Then the collection of ring
maps g′
α : S −→Wα is a formal open covering, too.
Proof. The argument, based on the fact that any base change of an open covering (of
discrete commutative rings) is an open covering, is straightforward.
□

2.9. Locality of Noetherianity. The following lemma is well-known.
Lemma 2.9.1. (a) Let f : R −→S be an open immersion of commutative rings.
Assume that the ring R is Noetherian. Then the ring S is Noetherian.
(b) Let f : R −→Sα be an open covering of a commutative ring R. Assume that
all the rings Sα are Noetherian. Then the ring R is Noetherian.
Proof. See, e. g., [14, Lemma Tag 01OW].
□
Recall from Section 2.1 that an adic topological ring R with an ideal of definition
I ⊂R is said to be adically Noetherian if the ring R/I is Noetherian.
Corollary 2.9.2. (a) Let f : R −→S be an formal open immersion of adic topological
rings. Assume that the ring R is adically Noetherian. Then the ring S is adically
Noetherian.
(b) Let f : R −→Sα be a formal open covering of an adic topological ring R.
Assume that all the adic topological rings Sα are adically Noetherian. Then the ring
R is adically Noetherian.
Proof. Follows from Lemma 2.9.1.
□
Lemma 2.9.3. Let R be an adic topological ring. Then the following conditions are
equivalent:
(1) the adic topological ring R is adically Noetherian;
(2) the complete, separated adic topological ring Λ(R) is adically Noetherian;
(3) the ring Λ(R) is Noetherian (as an abstract commutative ring).
In particular, a complete, separated adic topological ring R is adically Noetherian if
and only if it is Noetherian as an abstract commutative ring.
Proof. (1) ⇐⇒(2) Obvious.
(2) ⇐⇒(3) This is [4, Corollary 5 in Section III.2.10].
□
Corollary 2.9.4. (a) Let f : R −→S be an formal open immersion of adic topological
rings. Assume that the ring Λ(R) is Noetherian. Then the ring Λ(S) is Noetherian.
(b) Let f : R −→Sα be a formal open covering of an adic topological ring R.
Assume that all the rings Λ(Sα) are Noetherian. Then the ring Λ(R) is Noetherian.
Proof. Follows from Corollary 2.9.2 and Lemma 2.9.3.
□
2.10. Tightness and tautness. This section is largely based on [14, Section
Tag 0GX1] and [33, Sections 4–5].
The main result of the section is our version
of [14, Lemma Tag 0GBS] and [33, Lemmas 5.4 and 8.3].
In the following lemma, all the seven conditions are similar to the respective condi-
tions from Lemma 2.1.4, while conditions (5–6) are special cases of conditions (1–2)
from [33, Lemma 4.2].
Lemma 2.10.1. Let R and S be adic topological rings, and let f : R −→S be a ring
map. Then the following seven conditions are equivalent:
37


## Page 38


(1) there exist an ideal of definition I ⊂R and an ideal of definition J ⊂S such
that J is contained in the closure of the ideal Sf(I) ⊂S;
(2) for every ideal of definition J ⊂S there exist an ideal of definition I ⊂R and
an integer n ≥1 such that the ideal Jn is contained in the closure of the ideal
Sf(I) ⊂S;
(3) for any two ideals of definition I ⊂R and J ⊂S there exists an integer n ≥1
such that the ideal Jn is contained in the closure of the ideal Sf(I) ⊂S;
(4) for every ideal of definition I ⊂R there exists an ideal of definition J ⊂S
such that J is contained in the closure of the ideal Sf(I) ⊂S;
(5) for every open ideal I ⊂R, the closure J of the ideal Sf(I) ⊂S is an open
ideal in S;
(6) the open ideals I ⊂R for which the closure J of the ideal Sf(I) ⊂S is an
open ideal in S form a base of neighborhoods of zero in R;
(7) there exists an ideal of definition I ⊂R such that the closure J of the ideal
Sf(I) ⊂S is an open ideal in S.
Proof. (1) =⇒(5) Let I′ ⊂R and J′ ⊂S be ideals of definition such that J′ is
contained in the closure of the ideal Sf(I′) ⊂S. Then there exists an integer n ≥1
such that (I′)n ⊂I. Under any continuous map of topological spaces, the image of
the closure of a subset is contained in the closure of the image. Hence the closure of
the ideal Sf(I′)n ⊂S contains (J′)n, so the closure of Sf(I′)n is an open ideal in S.
Since Sf(I′)n ⊂Sf(I), it follows that the closure of Sf(I) is an open ideal in S, too.
(5) =⇒(3) =⇒(2) =⇒(1) and (3) =⇒(4) =⇒(1) Obvious.
(5) ⇐⇒(6) =⇒(7) ⇐⇒(1) Obvious.
□
We will say that a ring homomorphism f : R −→S acting between two adic
topological rings is a taut ring map if the equivalent conditions of Lemma 2.10.1 hold
(cf. [14, Section Tag 0GX1] and [33, Section 4]). Notice that a taut ring map need not
be continuous in our definition. Using the criterion of Lemma 2.10.1(4) or (5), one
can easily check that the composition of any two taut ring maps of adic topological
rings is taut.
In the following lemma, we do not say that “J is an ideal of definition in S”
because, in our terminology, an ideal of definition must be finitely generated.
Lemma 2.10.2. Let R and S be adic topological rings, and let f : R −→S be a ring
map. Then the following four conditions are equivalent:
(1) the ring homomorphism f is continuous and taut;
(2) there exists an ideal of definition I ⊂R such that the closure J of the ideal
Sf(I) ⊂S is an open ideal in S and the powers Jn of the ideal J form a base
of neighborhoods of zero in S;
(3) for every ideal of definition I ⊂R, the closure J of the ideal Sf(I) ⊂S is an
open ideal in S and the powers Jn of the ideal J form a base of neighborhoods
of zero in S;
(4) for every ideal of definition I ⊂R, the closure J of the ideal Sf(I) ⊂S is an
open ideal in S, for every integer n ≥1, the closure of the ideal Sf(In) ⊂S
38


## Page 39


coincides with the ideal Jn ⊂S, and the ideals Jn form a base of neighborhoods
of zero in S.
Proof. (2) =⇒(1) Assuming (2), the ring map f is taut by Lemma 2.10.1(1). Fur-
thermore, let J′ ⊂S be an ideal of definition. Then there exists n ≥1 such that
Jn ⊂J′. Hence f(In) ⊂Jn ⊂J′, and the map f is continuous by Lemma 2.1.3(2).
(4) =⇒(3) =⇒(2) Obvious.
(1) =⇒(4) By Lemma 2.1.3(4), there exists an ideal of definition J′ ⊂S such that
f(I) ⊂J′. Since J′ is an open ideal in S, we have J ⊂J′. By Lemma 2.10.1, J is
an open ideal in S. Hence Jn is an open ideal in S, too. In particular, Jn is a closed
ideal in S, and it follows that Jn is the closure of the ideal Sf(In) ⊂S. Since J ⊂J′,
the ideals Jn form a base of neighborhoods of zero in S.
□
Let A be a topological abelian group where open subgroups form a base of neigh-
borhoods of zero. Then the completion of A is the topological abelian group A =
Λ(A) = lim
←−U⊂A A/U. Here the projective limit is taken over the direct poset of open
subgroups U ⊂A, and the group A is endowed with the topology of projective limit
of discrete groups A/U. We denote by λA : A −→Λ(A) the natural (completion)
map.
Lemma 2.10.3. (a) Let A be a topological abelian group where open subgroups form
a base of neighborhoods of zero. Let B ⊂A be a subgroup. Then the closure of B is
open in A if and only if the closure of the subgroup λA(B) is open in Λ(A).
(b) Let R be an adic topological ring, and let I ⊂R be an ideal. Then the closure
of I is open in R if and only if the closure of the ideal Λ(R)λR(I) is open in the adic
topological ring Λ(R).
Proof. Part (a): the “if” assertion holds because the closure of B in A is the preimage
of the closure of λA(B) in Λ(A) under the natural (continuous) map λA. To prove the
“only if” assertion, one can observe that the preimage map V 7−→λ−1
A (V) establishes
a bijection between the closed subgroups in Λ(A) and the closed subgroups in A, as
well as between the open subgroups in Λ(A) and the open subgroups in A.
Part (b) follows from part (a), because the closure of the ideal Λ(R)λR(I) ⊂Λ(R)
coincides with the closure of the subgroup λR(I) ⊂Λ(R). The point is that I is an
ideal in R and Λ(R) is of its subring λR(R) ⊂Λ(R).
□
Obviously, any tight ring map between adic topological rings is taut. The coun-
terexample from Remark 2.3.2 above shows that a taut continuous ring map between
adic topological rings need not be tight. The following corollary should be also com-
pared with Remark 2.3.2.
Corollary 2.10.4. Let R and S be adic topological rings, and let f : R −→S be a
continuous ring map. Then the ring map f is taut if and only if the induced ring
map Λ(f): Λ(R) −→Λ(S) is taut.
Proof. Follows from Lemma 2.10.1(5) or (7) together with Lemma 2.10.3(b) (applied
to the adic topological ring S with the ideal Sf(I) ⊂S).
□

2.11. Locality of tautness and tightness. The following technical lemma will be
used in the proofs of Propositions 2.11.2 and 2.11.5.

Proposition 2.11.2. Let f : R −→S be a continuous homomorphism of adic topo-
logical rings, and let gα : S −→Tα be a formal open covering. Then the ring map f
is taut if and only if the composition gαf : R −→Tα is taut for every index α.
Proof. The “only if” assertion holds since the formal open immersions are tight (hence
taut) and the compositions of taut ring maps are taut. To prove the “if”, pick an
ideal of definition I ⊂R. Without loss of generality we can assume that the set of
indices α is finite. For every n ≥1 and every index α, denote by Kα,n the closure of
the ideal Tαgαf(In) ⊂Tα. By Lemma 2.10.1(5), the ideal Kα,n ⊂Tα is open.
Furthermore, denote by Jn ⊂S the intersection of the ideals g−1
α (Kα,n) ⊂S. Since
the maps gα are continuous and the set of indices α is finite, the ideal Jn is open
in S as well. Clearly, we have f(In) ⊂Jn, hence Kα,n is the closure of the ideal
Tαgα(Jn) ⊂Tα. As the map gα is tight, we actually have Kα,n = Tαgα(Jn).
Put R = R/In+1, S = S/Jn+1, and T α = Tα/Kα,n+1. Let I = In/In+1 ⊂R,
J = Jn/Jn+1 ⊂S, and Kα = Kα,n/Kα,n+1 ⊂T α be the related notation for ideals.
Then the collection of ring homomorphisms ¯gα : S −→T α is an open covering. Fur-
thermore, we have T α¯gα ¯f(I) = Kα = T α¯gα(J) (where ¯f is the ring homomorphism
R −→S). Since an ideal in S is determined by the collection of its extensions to T α,
it follows that J = S ¯f(I).
Thus Jn = Sf(In) + Jn+1 ⊂S. It remains to point that, for every index α, the
collection of ideals Kα,n is a base of neighborhoods of zero in Tα, by Lemma 2.10.2(4).
Hence the ideals Jn form a base of neighborhoods of zero in S, by Lemma 2.11.1.
Applying Lemma 2.10.6(2) or (3), we conclude that f is a taut ring map.
□

Corollary 2.11.3. Let f : R −→S be a continuous homomorphism of adic topolog-
ical rings, with a complete, separated adic topological ring S. Let gα : S −→Tα be
a formal open covering. Then the ring map f is tight if and only if the composition
gαf : R −→Tα is tight for every index α.
Proof. The “only if” implication holds since the formal open immersions are tight
and the compositions of tight ring maps are tight. To prove the “if” implication,
notice that tightness of the maps gαf obviously implies their tautness. By Proposi-
tions 2.11.2, it follows that the ring map f is taut. Applying Proposition 2.10.7, we
conclude that f is tight.
□
Lemma 2.11.4. Let R, S, and T be adic topological rings, let f : R −→S be a taut
continuous ring map, and let g: R −→T be a continuous ring map. Put W = T ⊗RS,
and denote by f ′ : T −→W and g′ : S −→W the induced ring maps. Endow the
commutative ring W with the tensor product topology, as in Section 2.7. Then the
continuous ring map f ′ is also taut.
Proof. By Corollary 2.10.4, the continuous ring map Λ(f): Λ(R) −→Λ(S) is
taut. By Proposition 2.10.7, it follows that the ring map Λ(f) is tight. Hence, by
Lemma 2.7.3(a), the ring map Λ(T) −→Λ(T) ⊗Λ(R) Λ(S) is tight, and consequently,
taut. By Lemma 2.7.4, we have Λ(W) = Λ(Λ(T)⊗Λ(R) Λ(S)). Using Corollary 2.10.4
again, we conclude that the ring map Λ(f ′): Λ(T) −→Λ(W) is taut. By the same
corollary, it follows that the ring map f ′ is taut.
□

2.12. Very flat and contraadjusted contramodules. In the sequel, we will use
a shorthand notation R–Contra = R–Modqs
ctra for any adic topological ring R. In
the case of a complete, separated adic topological ring R, a contramodule over R
as a topological ring is essentially the same thing as a quotseparated contramodule
R-module (see the end of Section 2.4), so this notation is unambiguous. Let us also
recall a similar notation R–Tors = R–Modtors from Section 2.2.
We start with a recollection of the case of an abstract commutative ring R (with-
out any adic or other topology). An R-module P is said to be contraadjusted [18,
Section 1.1], [39, Section 5], [21, Sections 2 and 8], [35, Section 0.5], [27, Section 2
and Example 3.2], [29, Section 4.3] if Ext1
R(R[s−1], P) = 0 for all elements s ∈R. An
R-module F is said to be very flat [18, Section 1.1], [39, Section 2], [21, Section 8],
[35, Section 0.5], [27, Section 2 and Example 2.5], [29, Section 4.3] if Ext1
R(F, C) = 0
for all contraadjusted R-modules C.
The full subcategory of contraadjusted R-modules is closed under extensions, quo-
tients, and infinite products in R–Mod. The full subcategory of very flat R-modules
is closed under extensions, kernels of epimorphisms, infinite direct sums, and tensor
products in R–Mod. All very flat R-modules have projective dimensions at most 1.
A homomorphism of commutative rings f : R −→S is said to be very flat if,
for every element s ∈S, the R-module S[s−1] is very flat. If this is the case, the
ring S is also said to be a very flat commutative R-algebra. Very flatness of the
R-module S is not sufficient for the ring homomorphism R −→S to be very flat [35,
Example 9.7], [29, Section 6.1]. The composition of any two very flat homomorphisms
of commutative rings is very flat [18, Lemma 1.2.3(b)]. Every open immersion of
commutative rings is very flat [18, Lemma 1.2.4].
44


## Page 45


Lemma 2.12.1. Let R be an adic topological ring and F be a contramodule R-module.
Then the following two conditions are equivalent:
(1) F is a flat contramodule R-module and there exists an ideal of definition I ⊂R
for which F/IF is a very flat R/IR-module;
(2) for every open ideal I ⊂R, the R/I-module F/IF is very flat.
Proof. (2) =⇒(1) holds because all very flat R/I-modules are flat.
(1) =⇒(2) Let I ⊂I′ ⊂R be two ideals of definition in R. Then I′/I is a finitely
generated nilpotent ideal in the ring R/I. Therefore, assuming that the R/I-module
F/IF is flat, the R/I-module F/IF is very flat if and only if the R/I′-module F/I′F
is very flat [18, Lemma 1.7.10(b)]. Now if I ⊂I′ ⊂R are two ideals such that I is
an ideal of definition, and the R/I-module F/IF is very flat, then the R/I′-module
F/I′F is very flat by [18, Lemma 1.2.2(b)].
□
Let R be an adic topological ring.
A contramodule R-module F is said to be
very flat if it satisfies the equivalent conditions of Lemma 2.12.1.
All projective
contramodule R-modules and all projective quotseparated contramodule R-modules
are very flat (see the proof of Lemma 2.5.5 (2) ⇒(3)). It follows from Lemma 2.5.9
that the full subcategory of very flat quotseparated contramodule R-modules is closed
under extensions and kernels of epimorphisms in R–Modqs
ctra. Furthermore, in view
of [18, Corollary E.1.10(a)], all very flat quotseparated contramodule R-modules have
projective dimensions at most 1 as objects of R–Modqs
ctra. These assertions are special
cases of [18, Corollary E.4.1].
Lemma 2.12.2. Let R be an adic topological ring and F be a flat R-module. Then,
for any quotseparated contramodule R-module P, there is a natural Ext1-adjunction
isomorphism of abelian groups/R-modules
Ext1
R(F, P) ≃Ext1
R–Contra(Λ(F), P).
Proof. By [15, Lemma 3.5 or Proposition 3.6], we have LiΛ(F) = 0 for all i > 0.
Therefore, for any short exact sequence of R-modules 0 −→M −→N −→F −→0,
the short sequence of quotseparated contramodule R-modules 0 −→L0Λ(M) −→
L0Λ(N) −→L0Λ(F) −→0 is exact.
Now we have a pair of adjoint functors between abelian categories
R–Contra /
/ R–Mod,
L0Λ
o
where the right adjoint functor R–Contra −→R–Mod is the identity inclusion. The
right adjoint functor is exact, while the object F ∈R–Mod is adjusted to the
left adjoint functor L0Λ in the sense stated in the previous paragraph.
By [27,
Lemma 1.7(e)], the desired Ext1-adjunction isomorphism follows. Here we are also
using the natural R-module isomorphism L0Λ(F) ≃Λ(F), which holds by the same
results from [15] or by Corollary 2.5.7 above.
□
Lemma 2.12.3. Let R be an adic topological ring, R be the adic completion of R, and
P be a quotseparated contramodule R-module. Then the following seven conditions
are equivalent:

(1) the functor HomR(−, P) takes short exact sequences of very flat quotseparated

ules/quotseparated contramodule R-modules;
(2) one has Ext1
R–Contra(F, P) = 0 for all very flat quotseparated contramod-
ule R-modules F (where the Ext group is computed in the abelian category
R–Contra = R–Modqs
ctra);
(3) one has Ext1
R–Contra(Λ(R[r−1]), P) = 0 for all elements r ∈R;
(4) P is a contraadjusted R-module;
(5) P is a contraadjusted R-module;
(6) for every ideal I ⊂R, the R/I-module P/IP is contraadjusted;
(7) for every ideal I ⊂R, the R/I-module P/IP is contraadjusted.
Furthermore, if P is a separated contramodule R-module, then conditions (1–7) are
also equivalent to the following two conditions:
(8) there exists an ideal of definition I ⊂R for which P/IP is a contraadjusted
R/I-module;
(9) for every open ideal I ⊂R, the R/I-module P/IP is contraadjusted.
Proof. (1) ⇐⇒(2) Follows from the facts that there are enough very flat quotsepa-
rated contramodules in R–Modqs
ctra and the full subcategory of very flat quotseparated
contramodules is closed under kernels of epimorphisms.
(3) ⇐⇒(4) Follows immediately from Lemma 2.12.2 (applied to the flat R-module
F = R[r−1]).
(1) ⇐⇒(4) This is a special case of [18, first assertion of Corollary E.4.8]. In the
case of a Noetherian ring R, one can also refer to [18, Corollary D.3.5(c,e)].
(1) ⇐⇒(5) This is a particular case of (1) ⇐⇒(4).
(6) =⇒(4) or (7) =⇒(5) Take I = 0.
(4) =⇒(6) or (5) =⇒(7) This is [18, Lemma 1.7.7(b)].
(6) =⇒(9) and (7) =⇒(9) =⇒(8) Obvious.
(8) =⇒(9) This is [18, Lemma 1.7.10(a)].
(9) =⇒(2) This is a special case of [18, Lemma E.4.3].

A quotseparated contramodule R-module P is said to be contraadjusted if it satis-
fies any one of the equivalent conditions (1–7) of Lemma 2.12.3. The full subcategory
of contraadjusted quotseparated contramodule R-modules is closed under extensions,
quotients, and infinite products in R–Modqs
ctra.
Proposition 2.12.4. Let R be an adic topological ring and M be a quotseparated
contramodule R-module. Then
(a) there exists a short exact sequence of quotseparated contramodule R-modules
0 −→P −→F −→M −→0 with a very flat quotseparated contramodule R-module
F and a contraadjusted quotseparated contramodule R-module P;
(b) there exists a short exact sequence of quotseparated contramodule R-modules
0 −→M −→P −→F −→0 with a contraadjusted quotseparated contramodule
R-module P and a very flat quotseparated contramodule R-module F.

can also refer to [18, Corollary D.3.5(b,e)]. Part (b) is a special case of [18, Corol-
lary E.4.6]. For a Noetherian ring R, one can also refer to [18, Corollary D.3.5(a,e)].

Corollary 2.12.5. Let R be an adic topological ring and F be a quotseparated con-
tramodule R-module. Then the following conditions are equivalent:
(1) the functor
HomR(F, −) takes short exact sequences of contraadjusted
quotseparated contramodule R-modules to short exact sequences of abelian
groups/R-modules/quotseparated contramodule R-modules;
(2) one has Ext1
R–Contra(F, P) = 0 for all contraadjusted quotseparated contramod-
ule R-modules P;
(3) F is a very flat quotseparated contramodule R-module.
Proof. (1) ⇐⇒(2) Follows from the facts that there are enough contraadjusted quot-
separated contramodules in R–Modqs
ctra (by Proposition 2.12.4(b)) and the full sub-
category of contraadjusted quotseparated contramodules is closed under cokernels of
monomorphisms.
(3) =⇒(2) Holds by Lemma 2.12.3(2).
(2) =⇒(3) This a standard argument. By Proposition 2.12.4(a), there exists a
short exact sequence 0 −→P −→G −→F −→0 in R–Modqs
ctra such that G is very
flat and P is contraadjusted. By (2), it follows that F is a direct summand of G.
It remains to make the obvious observation that the class of very flat quotseparated
contramodule R-modules is closed under direct summands. (For a Noetherian ring
R, the reference to [18, Corollary D.3.5(d,e)] is also applicable.)
□
The following lemma is the very flat counterpart of Corollary 2.6.4.
Lemma 2.12.6. Let f : R −→S be a continuous homomorphism of adic topological
rings. Then, for any very flat quotseparated contramodule R-module F, the quot-
separated contramodule S-module f ♯(F) is very flat. For any very flat contramodule
R-module F, the contramodule S-module f #(F) is very flat.
Proof. The argument is similar to the proof of Corollary 2.6.4 and based on
Lemma 2.6.3(b–c). The point is that, for any homomorphism of (abstract, nontopo-
logical) commutative rings ¯f : R −→S, the functor ¯f ∗: R–Mod −→S–Mod takes
very flat R-modules to very flat S-modules [18, Lemma 1.2.2(b)].
□
Lemma 2.12.7. Let f : R −→S be a continuous homomorphism of adic topological
rings. Then, for any contraadjusted quotseparated contramodule S-module P, the
quotseparated contramodule R-module f♯(P) is contraadjusted.
Proof. Using the criterion of Lemma 2.12.3(4), the question reduces to the case
of a homomorphism of abstract (nontopological) commutative rings, where we
can refer to [18, Lemma 1.2.2(a)]. Alternatively, an argument proceeding entirely
within the realm of quotseparated contramodules is possible, using the criterion of
Lemma 2.12.3(2) and the results of Corollary 2.6.5 and Lemma 2.12.6, and similar
to the proof of Lemma 2.13.4 sketched below.
□

The next lemma is a very flat complement to Lemma 2.1.8.
Lemma 2.12.8. Let f : R −→S be a tight continuous map of adic topological rings.
Then the following three conditions are equivalent:
(1) for every open ideal I ⊂R, the induced ring map ¯f : R/I −→S/Sf(I) is very
flat;
(2) there exists an ideal of definition I ⊂R such that the induced ring map
¯f : R/In −→S/Sf(In) is very flat for every n ≥1;
(3) f is flat (as a map of adic topological rings) and there exists an ideal of
definition I ⊂R such that the induced ring map ¯f : R/I −→S/Sf(I) is very
flat.
Proof. All the assertions follow from [18, Lemmas 1.2.2(b) and 1.7.10(b)].
□
We will say that a tight continuous homomorphism of adic topological rings
f : R −→S is very flat if it satisfies the equivalent conditions of Lemma 2.12.8. Any
formal open immersion of adic topological rings is very flat. Using the criterion of
Lemma 2.12.8(1), one can easily check that the composition of any two very flat
tight continuous ring maps of adic topological rings is very flat.
The following lemma is the very flat version of Lemma 2.3.1(b). The proof is the
same.

Lemma 2.12.12. Let R be an adic topological ring with an ideal of definition I ⊂R,
and let F be an R-module such that the R/In-module F/InF is very flat for all

Let 0 −→P −→M −→N −→0 be a short exact sequence of quot-
separated contramodule R-modules with a contraadjusted quotseparated contramod-
ule R-module P. Then the short sequence of quotseparated contramodule R-modules

actness of the short sequence in question follows from the fact that Ext1

The following lemma is a partial very flat version of Lemma 2.7.3(b).
Lemma 2.12.13. Let R, S, and T be adic topological rings, let f : R −→S be a very
flat tight continuous ring map, and let g: R −→T be a formal open immersion. Put

Endow the commutative ring W with the tensor product topology, as in Section 2.7.
Then the tight continuous ring map f ′ is very flat, too.
Proof. The point is that if R −→S is a very flat homomomorphism of commutative

homomorphism, too. This is a special case of [18, Lemma 1.10.1].

2.13. Cotorsion contramodules. Once again, we start with a reminder of the case
of an abstract associative ring R. A left R-module P is said to be cotorsion (in the
sense of Enochs [6]) if Ext1
R(F, P) = 0 for all flat left R-modules P. The full subcate-
gory of cotorsion R-modules is closed under extensions, cokernels of monomorphisms,
and infinite products in R–Mod.
Lemma 2.13.1. Let R be an adic topological ring and P be a quotseparated contra-
module R-module. Then the following three conditions are equivalent:
(1) the functor HomR(−, P) takes short exact sequences of flat quotseparated
contramodule R-modules to short exact sequences of abelian groups/R-mod-
ules/quotseparated contramodule R-modules;
(2) one has Ext1
R–Contra(F, P) = 0 for all flat quotseparated contramodule R-mod-
ules F;
(3) one has Extn
R–Contra(F, P) = 0 for all flat quotseparated contramodule R-mod-
ules F and all integers n ≥1.
Proof. All the equivalences follow from the facts that there are enough flat quotsep-

ctra and the full subcategory of flat quotseparated
contramodules is closed under kernels of epimorphisms (see Lemma 2.5.9(a)).

Lemma 2.13.6. Let R be a Noetherian commutative ring endowed with an adic
topology, R be the adic completion of R, and P be a contramodule R-module. Then
the following conditions are equivalent:
(1) P is a cotorsion (quotseparated) contramodule R-module;
(2) P is a cotorsion R-module;
(3) P is a cotorsion R-module.

groups/modules, by Proposition 2.5.12. In view of these observations, the equivalence
(1) ⇐⇒(2) is provided by [18, Corollary D.2.8(c)]. The equivalence (1) ⇐⇒(3) is a
particular case of (1) ⇐⇒(2).
□
2.14. Locality of flatness and very flatness of morphisms. This section is a
continuation of Sections 2.10–2.11.
The following lemma is to be compared with Lemma 2.1.8.
Lemma 2.14.1. Let f : R −→S be a taut continuous map of adic topological rings.
Then the following two conditions are equivalent:
(1) there exists an ideal of definition I ⊂R such that, denoting by Jn the closure
of the ideal Sf(In) ⊂S, the R/In-module S/Jn is flat for every n ≥1;
(2) for every open ideal I ⊂R, denoting by J the closure of the ideal Sf(I) ⊂S,
the R/I-module S/J is flat.
Proof. (2) =⇒(1) Obvious.
(1) =⇒(2) Let I′ ⊂I be an open ideal such that, denoting by J′ the closure of the
ideal Sf(I′) ⊂S, the R/I′-module S/J′ is flat. We need to show that the R/I-module
S/J is flat whenever I′ ⊂I. Indeed, J′ is an open ideal in S by Lemma 2.10.1(5).
Since J′ ⊂J, it follows that J = Sf(I) + J′. Thus S/J ≃R/I ⊗R/I′ S/J′, and the
assertion follows.
□
We will say that a taut continuous homomorphism of adic topological rings
f : R −→S is taut-flat if it satisfies the equivalent conditions of Lemma 2.14.1.
Notice that a tight continuous map of adic topological rings is taut-flat if and only
if it is flat. Using the criterion of Lemma 2.14.1(2), one can easily check that the
composition of any two taut-flat continuous ring maps of adic topological rings is
taut-flat.
The next lemma is to be compared with Lemma 2.12.8.
51


## Page 52


Lemma 2.14.2. Let f : R −→S be a taut continuous map of adic topological rings.
Then the following three conditions are equivalent:
(1) for every open ideal I ⊂R, denoting by J the closure of the ideal Sf(I) ⊂S,
the induced ring map ¯f : R/I −→S/J is very flat;
(2) there exists an ideal of definition I ⊂R such that, denoting by Jn the closure
of the ideal Sf(In) ⊂S, the induced ring map ¯f : R/In −→S/Jn is very flat
for every n ≥1;
(3) f is taut-flat and there exists an ideal of definition I ⊂R such that, denoting
by J the closure of the ideal Sf(I) ⊂S, the induced ring map ¯f : R/I −→S/J
is very flat.
Proof. (1) =⇒(2) =⇒(3) Obvious.
(2) =⇒(1) Let I′ ⊂I be an open ideal such that, denoting by J′ the closure of
the ideal Sf(I′) ⊂S, the ring map R/I′ −→S/J′ is very flat. We need to show
that the ring map R/I −→S/J is very flat whenever I′ ⊂I. Following the proof of
Lemma 2.14.1, we have S/J ≃R/I ⊗R/I′ S/J′. It remains to point out that the ring
maps S/J′ −→S/J is surjective (so every localization of S/J at an element arises
from a localization of S/J′ at an element), and refer to [18, Lemma 1.2.2(b)].
(3) =⇒(2) We have S/J ≃R/I ⊗R/In S/Jn, so [18, Lemma 1.7.10(b)] is applicable.
□
We will say that a taut continuous homomorphism of adic topological rings
f : R −→S is taut-very flat if it satisfies the equivalent conditions of Lemma 2.14.2.
Notice that a tight continuous map of adic topological rings is taut-very flat if and
only if it is very flat.
Using the criterion of Lemma 2.14.2(1), one can easily check that the composition of
any two taut-very flat continuous ring maps of adic topological rings is taut-very flat.
The fact that the composition of any two very flat homomorphisms of commutative
rings is very flat (see Section 2.12) needs to be used here.
The following lemma is a generalization of Lemmas 2.3.1(b) and 2.12.9.
Lemma 2.14.3. Let f : R −→S be a continuous map of adic topological rings. In
this context:
(a) the map f is taut-flat if and only if the map Λ(f) is flat;
(b) the map f is taut-very flat if and only if the map Λ(f) is very flat.
Proof. First of all, the map f is taut if and only if the map Λ(f) is tight, by Corol-
lary 2.10.4 and Proposition 2.10.7. Assume that this is the case. Put R = Λ(R) and
S = Λ(S), and denote by f = Λ(f) the induced ring map R −→S. Let I ⊂R be an
open ideal and I ⊂R be the related open ideal, as per the discussion in Section 2.3.
Let J ⊂S be the closure of the ideal Sf(I) ⊂S. Then J = SλS(J) is the open ideal
in S corresponding to J. Clearly, J is the closure of the ideal Sf(I) ⊂S. Since the
map f is tight, it follows that J = Sf(I). Now we have R/I ≃R/I and S/J ≃S/J.
Both the assertions of parts (a) and (b) follow.
□
52


## Page 53


Lemma 2.14.4. Let f : R −→S be a continuous homomorphism of adic topological
rings. Let gα : S −→Tα be a formal open covering. In this context:
(a) the map f is taut-flat if and only if the composition gαf : R −→Tα is taut-flat
for every index α;
(b) the map f is taut-very flat if and only if the composition gαf : R −→Tα is
taut-very flat for every index α.
Proof. The “only if” assertions hold, since the maps gα are taut-very flat, the com-
positions of taut-flat morphisms are taut-flat, and the compositions of taut-very flat
morphisms are taut-very flat. To prove the “if”, we use the notation from the proof of
Proposition 2.11.2. According to that proof, we have Jn = Sf(In) + Jn+1 ⊂S, hence
Jn is the closure of the ideal Sf(In) ⊂S by the proof of Lemma 2.10.6 (2) ⇒(1). We
also know that the ring homomorphisms ¯gα form an open covering.
Part (a): we know that T α are flat R-modules, and need to prove that S is a flat
R-module. This is a standard fact from commutative algebra; see, e. g., [14, Lemma
Tag 01U5] or [18, Lemma 1.8.2].
Part (b): we know that the ring homomorphisms ¯gα ¯f are very flat, and need to
prove that the ring homomorphism ¯f is very flat. This result can be found in [18,
Lemma 1.8.6].
□
Corollary 2.14.5. Let f : R −→S be a continuous homomorphism of adic topological
rings. Let gα : S −→Tα be a formal open covering. In this setting:
(a) the map Λ(f) is flat (as a map of adic topological rings) if and only if the map
Λ(gαf) is flat for every index α;
(a) the map Λ(f) is very flat (as a map of adic topological rings) if and only if the
map Λ(gαf) is very flat for every index α.
Proof. Follows from Lemmas 2.14.3 and 2.14.4.
□
Lemma 2.14.6. Let R, S, and T be adic topological rings, and let f : R −→S and
g: R −→T be a continuous ring maps. Put W = T ⊗RS, and denote by f ′ : T −→W
and g′ : S −→W the induced ring maps. Endow the commutative ring W with the
tensor product topology, as in Section 2.7. In this setting:
(a) if the ring map f is taut-flat, then the continuous ring map f ′ is also taut-flat;
(b) if the ring map f is taut-very flat and the ring map g is a formal open immer-
sion, then the continuous ring map f ′ is also taut-very flat.
Proof. Part (a): by Lemma 2.14.3(a), the continuous ring map Λ(f): Λ(R) −→Λ(S)
is flat. Hence, by Lemma 2.7.3(b), the ring map Λ(T) −→Λ(T) ⊗Λ(R) Λ(S) is flat,
and consequently, taut-flat. By Lemma 2.7.4, we have Λ(W) = Λ(Λ(T) ⊗Λ(R) Λ(S)).
Using Lemma 2.14.3(a) again, we conclude that the ring map Λ(f ′): Λ(T) −→Λ(W)
is flat. By the same lemma, it follows that the ring map f ′ is taut-flat.
The proof of part (b) is similar and based on Lemmas 2.14.3(b) and 2.12.13.
□

2.15. The coextension of scalars is quotseparated. The aim of this section is
to formulate and prove quotseparated versions of Lemmas 2.6.6(b) and 2.6.7(b). Our
exposition is based on the ideas and techniques from the new preprint [33].
For any adic topological ring R with an ideal of definition I ⊂R and any R-module
M, we will denote the kernel of the I-adic completion map λI,M : M −→ΛI(M) by
ΩR(M) = T
n≥1 InM ⊂M.
Lemma 2.15.1. Let R and S be adic topological rings, and let f : R −→S be a tight
continuous ring map. Then an S-module Q is separated and complete if and only if
the R-module f∗(Q) is separated and complete. Moreover, for any S-module N, one
has ΩR(N) = ΩS(N) ⊂N. Denoting by I ⊂R and J ⊂S any ideals of definition in
R and S, we also have a natural isomorphism of R-modules ΛI(N) ≃ΛJ(N) forming
a commutative triangular diagram with the completion maps λI,N : N −→ΛI(N) and
λJ,N : N −→ΛJ(N).
Proof. All the assertions follow immediately from Lemma 2.1.6(4).
□
Let f : R −→S be a tight continuous map of adic topological rings.
With
Lemma 2.15.1 in mind, we will write simply Ω(N) instead of ΩR(N) = ΩS(N),
and Λ(N) instead of ΛI(N) ≃ΛJ(N), for any S-module N. The completion map
N −→Λ(N) will be denoted, as usual, by λN.

Theorem 2.15.2. Let R and S be adic topological rings, and let f : R −→S be a flat

and only if the R-module f∗(Q) is a quotseparated contramodule.

module. Then Q is a contramodule S-module by Lemma 2.6.6(b). We need to pove
that Q is a quotseparated contramodule S-module.

completion map λQ : Q −→Λ(Q) is surjective, and we have a short exact sequence
of S-modules 0 −→Ω(Q) −→Q −→Λ(Q) −→0.
Pick a flat quotseparated contramodule S-module F together with a surjective
S-module map F −→Λ(Q).
For example, it suffices to take a free S-module F
together with a surjective S-module morphism F −→Λ(Q), and put F = Λ(F).
Consider the pullback diagram in the category of S-modules

By Lemma 2.6.9(d), the underlying R-module of F is a flat quotseparated contra-

well as a separated contramodule S-module). Applying the functor Λ and the natural

of that map as the composition of surjective S-module maps

contramodule R-module, Corollary 2.5.8 is applicable, and it follows that Ω(Q1) = 0.
Hence Ω(Q) = 0.
Now we proceed by transfinite induction, iterating the construction above at the
successor steps, and passing to the projective limit at the limit steps. Let ℵbe a

a projective system of S-modules Qi, 0 ≤i < ℵ, and surjective S-module morphisms

will be a proper embedding (i. e., not an isomorphism) whenever Ω(Qi) ̸= 0.

contramodule S-module Fi together with a surjective S-module map Fi −→Λ(Qi).

Fi −→Λ(Qi). For every limit ordinal j < ℵ, put Qj = lim

The only property we still need to check is that, for a limit ordinal j, the map

After the construction is finished, we observe that a set Q of the cardinality less
than ℵcannot have a descending chain of subsets of length ℵ. Hence there exists an
ordinal k < ℵfor which the map Ω(Qk+1) −→Ω(Qk) is an isomorphism. As we have
shown above, it follows that Ω(Qk) = 0.
Thus Qk is an S-module whose underlying R-module is separated and complete.
By Lemma 2.15.1, it follows that Qk is separated and complete as an S-module. Thus
the contramodule S-module Q is a quotient S-module of a separated and complete
S-module Qk, so Q is a quotseparated contramodule S-module.

Remark 2.15.3. Notice that the assertion of Theorem 2.15.2 with the flatness
condition on f replaced by the tightness condition (i. e., the obvious version of
Lemma 2.6.6(b) for quotseparated contramodules) is not true. Indeed, let S be an
adic topological ring with an ideal of definition J such that there exists a nonquotsep-
arated contramodule S-module Q (see Section 2.4). Let s1, . . . , sm ∈J be a finite set
of generators of the ideal J. Consider the ring of polynomials R = Z[x1, . . . , xm] in
m variables over the ring of integers Z, and let f : R −→S be the ring homomorphism
taking xj to sj for all 1 ≤j ≤m. Let I = (x1, . . . , xm) ⊂R be the ideal generated by
the elements x1, . . . , xm. Endow R with the I-adic topology. Then f : R −→S is a
tight continuous ring map by Lemma 2.1.6(4), and f∗(Q) is a contramodule R-module
by Lemma 2.6.1(b). Since the ring R is Noetherian, all contramodule R-modules are
quotseparated; so f∗(Q) is a quotseparated contramodule R-module. But Q is not a
quotseparated contramodule S-module.
On the other hand, the conclusion of Theorem 2.15.2 certainly does not imply
flatness of the map f, i. e., the assertion of the theorem holds true for some nonflat
(but still tight) ring maps f : R −→S. For example, if both the rings R and S are
Noetherian and the map f is tight and continuous, then the assertion of the theorem
holds by Lemma 2.6.1(b) or (d) and Lemma 2.6.6(b). Alternatively, the assertion of
the theorem trivially holds for any two discrete (i. e., (0)-adic) rings R and S, and
56


## Page 57


any ring homomorphism f (since any module over a discrete commutative ring is a
separated, hence quotseparated, contramodule).
Lemma 2.15.4. Let R and S be adic topological rings, and let f : R −→S be a
tight continuous ring map. Then, for any separated contramodule R-module P, the
S-module f !(P) = HomR(S, P) is also a separated contramodule.
First proof. By Lemma 2.4.2(b), the underlying R-module of HomR(S, P) is sepa-
rated and complete. By Lemma 2.15.1, it follows that the S-module HomR(S, P) is
separated and complete as well.

proof of Proposition 10.3].

Finally, HomR/In(S/Jn, P/InP) is an S/Jn-module. So the S-module HomR(S, −)
is a projective limit of S/Jn-modules. It remains to point out that all S/Jn-modules
are separated contramodule S-modules, and the full subcategory of separated and
the full subcategory of separated contramodule S-modules is closed under all limits
in S–Mod.
The latter assertion holds since the full subcategory of contramodule
S-modules is closed under limits in S–Mod, while the full subcategory of separated
S-modules is closed under submodules and infinite products.

Proposition 2.15.5. Let R and S be adic topological rings, and let f : R −→S be a
flat tight continuous ring map. Then, for any quotseparated contramodule R-module
P, the S-module f !(P) = HomR(S, P) is also a quotseparated contramodule.
First proof. By Lemma 2.4.2(c), the underlying R-module of HomR(S, P) is a quot-
separated contramodule R-module. By Theorem 2.15.2, it follows that HomR(S, P)
is a quotseparated contramodule S-module.
□
Second proof. The following argument is a special case of [33, proof of Theorem 10.4].
The S-module HomR(S, P) is a contramodule by Lemma 2.6.7(b); we only have to
prove that it is a quotseparated contramodule.
By Proposition 2.13.2, there ex-
ists a short exact sequence of quotseparated contramodule R-modules 0 −→C −→
F −→P −→0 with a flat quotseparated contramodule R-module F and a co-
torsion quotseparated contramodule R-module C.
By Lemma 2.13.5 applied to
the R-module F = S, the short sequence of S-modules 0 −→HomR(S, C) −→
HomR(S, F) −→HomR(S, P) −→0 is exact. Now the contramodule R-module F is
separated by Lemma 2.5.6, hence the contramodule S-module HomR(S, P) is sepa-
rated by Lemma 2.15.4. Thus the contramodule S-module HomR(S, P) is a quotient
S-module of a separated contramodule S-module HomR(S, F), as desired.
□

of coextension of scalars f ! : R–Mod −→S–Mod restricts to a functor
f ! : R–Modqs

ctra,
which we will also call the coextension of scalars. The functor f ! is right adjoint to
the functor of restriction of scalars f♯: S–Modqs

ctra.

ule R-module P. Then the short sequence of quotseparated contramodule S-modules
0 −→f !P −→f !M −→f !N −→0 is exact.
Proof. This is the particular case of Lemma 2.12.12 for the R-module F = S.
□

We recall the notation IM = HomR(R/I, M) for the submodule of all elements in
an R-module M annihilated by an ideal I ⊂R.
Lemma 2.16.5. Let I be a finitely generated ideal in a commutative ring R. In this
context:
(a) Let M be an I-torsion R-module. Assume that IM = 0. Then M = 0.
(b) Let P be an I-contramodule R-module. Assume that P = IP. Then P = 0.
Proof. Part (a) is obvious. Part (b) is a very basic example of an assertion from the
class of results known as “contramodule Nakayama lemmas”. See [21, Lemma 4.2 and
Remark 4.3], which are applicable by [21, Theorems 3.3 and 4.1]. A simple argument
by induction on the number of generators of I, using the passage from P to P/sP
(where s ∈I is one of the generators), is also possible.
□
Lemma 2.16.6. Let R be an adic topological ring with an ideal of definition I ⊂R.
In this context:
(a) Let 0 −→K0 −→K1 −→K2 −→· · · be a bounded below complex of injective
torsion R-modules. Then the complex K• is acyclic if and only if the complex of
R/I-modules HomR(R/I, K•) is acyclic.
(b) Let · · · −→F2 −→F1 −→F0 −→0 be a bounded above complex of flat
quotseparated contramodule R-modules. Then the complex F• is acyclic if and only
if the complex of R/I-modules R/I ⊗R F• is acyclic.
Proof. Part (a): the “only if” assertion holds since any acyclic bounded below com-
plex of injective objects (in an abelian/exact category) is contractible. To prove the
“if”, let n ≥0 be the minimal integer for which the complex K• is not exact at
the cohomological degree n. Then we have a finite contractible complex of injective
torsion R-modules 0 −→K0 −→K1 −→· · · −→Kn−1 −→Kn −→Jn −→0, where
Jn is the cokernel of the differential Kn−1 −→Kn. Furthermore, the natural map
Jn −→Kn+1 is not injective. Now IJn is the cokernel of the map IKn−1 −→IKn,
while Lemma 2.16.5(a) implies that the map IJn −→IKn+1 cannot be injective.
Part (b): the “only if” assertion holds by Lemma 2.5.9. To prove the “if”, let n ≥0
be the minimal integer for which the complex F• is not exact at the homological
59


## Page 60


degree n. Then we have a finite exact complex of flat quotseparated contramodule
R-modules 0 −→Gn −→Fn −→Fn−1 −→· · · −→F1 −→F0 −→0, where Gn is the
kernel of the differential Fn −→Fn−1. Furthermore, the natural map Fn+1 −→Gn
is not surjective. Now it follows from Lemma 2.5.9 that R/I ⊗R Gn is the kernel of
the map R/I ⊗R Fn −→R/I ⊗R Fn−1, while Lemma 2.16.5(b) implies that the map
R/I ⊗R Fn+1 −→R/I ⊗R Gn cannot be surjective.
□

2.17. Colocality of exactness. Let R be an adic topological ring. We will denote
by R–Contracta ⊂R–Contra = R–Modqs
ctra the full subcategory of contraadjusted quot-
separated contramodule R-modules. Similarly, the notation R–Contracot ⊂R–Contra
stands for the full subcategory of cotorsion quotseparated contramodule R-modules.
As mentioned in Sections 2.12 and 2.13, the full subcategories R–Contracta and
R–Contracot are closed under extensions, cokernels of monomorphisms, and infi-
nite products in R–Contra.
(The full subcategory R–Contracta is even closed un-
der quotients.) Thus these full subcategories inherit exact category structures from
the abelian exact structure on R–Contra.
We will always view R–Contracta and
R–Contracot as exact categories with the exact structures inherited from (the abelian
exact structure on) R–Contra.
We will also denote by R–Contrafl ⊂R–Contra the full subcategory of flat quotsep-
arated contramodule R-modules, and by R–Contravfl ⊂R–Contra the full subcategory
of very flat quotseparated contramodule R-modules. According to Lemma 2.5.9(a)
and the discussion in Section 2.12, the full subcategories R–Contrafl and R–Contravfl
are closed under extensions and kernels of epimorphisms in R–Contra. Thus these full
subcategories also inherit exact category structures from the abelian exact structure
on R–Contra. We will always view R–Contrafl and R–Contravfl as exact categories
with the exact structures inherited from R–Contra.
For any abstract (nontopological) commutative ring R, the similar notation
R–Modcta ⊂R–Mod and R–Modvfl ⊂R–Mod stands for the exact categories of
contraadjusted and very flat R-modules. For an associative ring R, we denote by
R–Modcot ⊂R–Mod and R–Modfl ⊂R–Mod the exact subcategories of cotorsion
and flat left R-modules in the abelian category R–Mod.

Lemma 2.17.4. Let R be an adic topological ring with an ideal of definition I ⊂R.
In this setting:
(a) Let g: M −→N be a morphism of torsion R-modules. Then the map g is
injective if and only if the induced map ¯g: IM −→IN is injective.
(b) Let g: P −→Q be a morphism of contramodule R-modules. Then the map g is
surjective if and only if the induced map ¯g: P/IP −→Q/IQ is surjective.
Proof. Follows immediately from Lemma 2.16.5.
□

1 ≤α1 < · · · < αk ≤N, endow the rings Sα1,...,αk with the tensor product topologies,
and denote the induced ring maps by fα1,...,αk : R −→Sα1,...,αk.

Notice that the obvious analogues of Lemmas 2.17.9(b), 2.17.10(b), and 2.17.11(b)
for admissible (or split) monomorphisms are not true (not even for discrete regular
Noetherian commutative rings R and Sα of Krull dimension 1). The counterexample
can be discerned from [18, Example 3.2.1].
Proposition 2.17.12. Let R be an adic topological ring. In this context:
(a) a complex of contraadjusted quotseparated contramodule R-modules P• is exact
in R–Contracta if and only if it is exact in R–Contra;
(b) a complex of cotorsion quotseparated contramodule R-modules P• is exact in
R–Contracot if and only if it is exact in R–Contra.
Proof. Part (a) is easy: the assertion follows from the fact that the full subcate-
gory R–Contracta is closed under quotients in R–Contra. Part (b) is the cotorsion
periodicity theorem for contramodules; see [28, Corollary 12.4].
□
69


## Page 70


3. Contraherent Cosheaves of Contramodules
3.1. The basics. Let X be a locally Noetherian formal scheme and O = OX be its
structure sheaf. For every affine open formal subscheme U ⊂X, the ring of functions
R = OX(U) is a Noetherian commutative ring endowed with a complete, separated
adic topology (in the sense of Section 2.1).
The results collected in Section 2.9
are relevant to the fact that the notion of a (locally) Noetherian formal scheme is
reasonably well-behaved.
From this point on, by an adic Noetherian ring we will mean a Noetherian com-
mutative ring endowed with adic topology. So the adic Noetherian rings are the adic
topological rings R whose underlying abstract ring R is Noetherian.
An ideal I ⊂R is said to be an ideal of definition of a topological commutative ring
R if the topology of R is the I-adic topology. This terminology was discussed already
in Section 2.1. The ideals of definition I ⊂R are characterized by two properties:
for every integer n ≥1, the ideal In is open in R; and the descending sequence In,
n ≥1, of powers of the ideal I is a base of open neighborhoods of zero in R.
Closed subschemes of definition X ⊂X correspond bijectively to coherent sheaves
of ideals of definition.
The latter term means coherent sheaves of ideals I on X
such that, for every affine open formal subscheme U ⊂X, the ideal I(U) ⊂OX(U)
is an ideal of definition in the adic Noetherian ring R = OX(U). In particular, the
minimal closed subscheme of definition Xmn ⊂X corresponds to the quasi-coherent
sheaf formed by the maximal ideals of definition Imx ⊂OX(U).
Let V ⊂X be another affine open formal subscheme in X such that V ⊂U.
Put S = OX(V). Then we have the restriction homomorphism of topological rings
f : R −→S. For every ideal of definition I ⊂R, the extension J = Sf(I) of the ideal
I to the ring S is an ideal of definition J ⊂S. So f : R −→S is a tight continuous
ring map in the sense of Section 2.1. The discrete quotient ring S = S/J is the ring
of regular functions on an affine open subscheme Spec S in the affine scheme Spec R,
where R = R/I. In other words, f : R −→S is a formal open immersion of adic
topological rings in the sense of Section 2.8.
If U = S
α Vα is an affine open covering of an affine formal scheme U and I ⊂R
is an ideal of definition in R = O(U), then the collection of affine open subschemes
Spec Sα is an affine open covering of the affine scheme Spec R.
Here R = R/I
and Sα = Sα/Jα, where Jα = Sαfα(I) denotes the extension of the ideal I to the
ring Sα = OU(Vα). The notation fα stands for the restriction homomorphism of
topological rings fα : R −→Sα. So the collection of ring maps fα : R −→Sα is a
formal open covering of the adic topological ring R in the sense of Section 2.8.
Conversely, to any complete, separated adic Noetherian ring R, the affine Noe-
therian formal scheme Spf R (the formal spectrum of R) is naturally assigned. The
contravariant functor Spf establishes an anti-equivalence between the category of
complete, separated adic Notherian rings (and continuous ring homomorphisms) and
the category of affine Noetherian formal schemes.
70


## Page 71


Under the anti-equivalence of categories from the previous paragraph, open im-
mersions of affine Noetherian formal schemes Spf S −→Spf R correspond precisely
to formal open immersions of complete, separated adic Noetherian rings R −→S.
Affine open coverings of affine Noetherian formal schemes Spf Sα −→Spf R cor-
respond precisely to formal open coverings of complete, separated adic Noetherian
rings (by complete, separated adic Noetherian rings).
The abelian category of coherent sheaves on U = Spf R is naturally equivalent
to the abelian category of finitely generated R-modules. The closed subschemes of
definition U ⊂U correspond bijectively to the ideals of definition I ⊂R; the rule
is U = Spec R/I. Arbitrary closed subschemes U
′ ⊂U correspond bijectively to the
open ideals I′ ⊂R; once again, the rule is U
′ = Spec R/I′.
A locally Noetherian formal scheme X is said to be Noetherian if X is quasi-compact
(i. e., the underlying topological space of X is quasi-compact). A locally Noetherian
formal scheme X is said to be semi-separated if the intersection of any two affine open
formal subschemes in X is an affine open formal subscheme. Notice that every locally
Noetherian formal subschemes X is quasi-separated in the sense that the intersection
of any two quasi-compact open formal subschemes in X is quasi-compact.
Let X be a locally Noetherian formal scheme and X ⊂X be a closed subscheme of
definition in X. Then the formal scheme X is affine if and only if the scheme X is affine.
Indeed, the underlying topological spaces of X and X coincide; hence without loss
of generality we can assume X and X to be quasi-compact (i. e., Noetherian). Then
the assertion follows from the fact that, for any Noetherian scheme X with a closed
subscheme X, the scheme X is affine if and only if X is affine [13, Exercise III.3.1],
[14, Lemma Tag 01YQ].
Consequently, a locally Noetherian formal scheme X is semi-separated if and only
if it has a semi-separated closed subscheme of definition X, or equivalently, if and
only if every closed subscheme of definition in X is semi-separated.
Given an open covering W of a locally Noetherian formal scheme X, an open
formal subscheme Y ⊂X is said to be subordinate to W if there exists an open
formal subscheme W ∈W for which Y ⊂W. An open covering X = S
α Yα is said
to be subordinate to W if Yα is subordinate to W for every α. More generally, the
same terminology is applied to open coverings of arbitrary topological spaces.
3.2. Cosheaves of modules over a ringed space. Let (X, O) be a ringed space
and B be a base of open subsets in X.
Recall the definitions of a copresheaf of
O-modules on B and a cosheaf of O-modules on B from [18, Section 2.1] and [30,
Section 2.1].
Let us start with (pre)sheaves. The set B is partially ordered by inclusion, and
every poset can be viewed as a category. A presheaf of abelian groups M on B is a
contravariant functor M: Bop −→Ab (where Ab = Z–Mod is the category of abelian
groups). A presheaf of O-modules M on B is a presheaf of abelian groups such that,
for every open subset U ⊂X, U ∈B, an O(U)-module structure is defined on the
abelian group M(U). It is required that the restriction map M(U) −→M(V) be an
O(U)-module morphism for every pair of open subsets V ⊂U ⊂X, V, U ∈B.
71


## Page 72


A presheaf of abelian groups M on B is called a sheaf if the following sheaf axiom
is satisfied. Let U ⊂X, U ∈B be an open subset, U = S
α Vα be an open covering
of U by open subsets Vα ∈B, and let Vα ∩Vβ = S
γ Wαβγ be open coverings of the
intersections Vα ∩Vβ by open subsets Wαβγ ∈B. Then the short sequence
(11)

Y

Y
α,β,γ M(Wαβγ)
should be left exact. A presheaf of O-modules M on B is called a sheaf of O-modules
if the underlying presheaf of abelian groups of M is a sheaf of abelian groups on B.
We denote the additive category of sheaves of O-modules on B by (B, O)–Sh. In
the case of sheaves of O-modules defined an all the open subsets of X (i. e., when
the base of open subsets under consideration consists of all the open subsets in X),
we denote the corresponding category of sheaves by (X, O)–Sh.
The main result in connection with the definition of a sheaf of O-modules on X is
the following theorem from [12, Section 0.3.2], [14, Lemma Tag 009U], [18, Propo-
sition 2.1.3], [30, Theorem 2.1(a)]: the natural restriction functor (X, O)–Sh −→
(B, O)–Sh is a category equivalence. So any sheaf of O-modules on B can be ex-
tended to a sheaf of O-modules on X in a unique way.
Now we turn to co(pre)sheaves. A copresheaf of abelian groups P on B is a covari-
ant functor P: B −→Ab. We denote by P[U] the abelian group that the copresheaf
P assigns to an open subset U ⊂X, U ∈B. The group P[U] is called the group
of cosections of the copresheaf P over an open subset U. The abelian group map
P[V] −→P[U] that the copresheaf P assigns to an identity inclusion V ⊂U of open
subsets V, U ∈B is called the corestriction map.
A copresheaf of O-modules P on B is a copresheaf of abelian groups such that,
for every open subset U ⊂X, U ∈B, an O(U)-module structure is defined on the
abelian group P[U]. It is required that the corestriction map P[V] −→P[U] be an
O(U)-module morphism for every pair of open subsets V ⊂U ⊂X, V, U ∈B.
A copresheaf of abelian groups P on B is called a cosheaf if the following cosheaf
axiom is satisfied. As above, let U ⊂X, U ∈B be an open subset, U = S
α Vα be an
open covering of U by open subsets Vα ∈B, and let Vα ∩Vβ = S
γ Wαβγ be open
coverings of the intersections Vα ∩Vβ by open subsets Wαβγ ∈B. Then the short
sequence

should be right exact. A copresheaf of O-modules P on B is called a cosheaf of
O-modules if the underlying copresheaf of abelian groups of P is a cosheaf of abelian
groups on B.
We denote the additive category of cosheaves of O-modules on B by (B, O)–Cosh.
In the case of cosheaves of O-modules defined an all the open subsets of X (i. e.,
when the base of open subsets under consideration consists of all the open subsets
in X), we denote the corresponding category of cosheaves by (X, O)–Cosh.
The main result in connection with the definition of a cosheaf of O-modules on X
is the following theorem from [18, Theorem 2.1.2], [30, Theorem 2.1(b)]: the natural

restriction functor (X, O)–Cosh −→(B, O)–Cosh is a category equivalence. So any
cosheaf of O-modules on B can be extended to a cosheaf of O-modules on X in a
unique way.

of O|W-modules on W. The restriction of a (co)sheaf of O-modules on X to W is a
(co)sheaf of O|W-modules on W.

3.3. Cosheaves of contramodules over a formal scheme. For any Noetherian
commutative ring R with an adic topology, we denote by R–Tors = R–Modtors ⊂
R–Mod the full subcategory of torsion R-modules and by R–Contra = R–Modqs
ctra =
R–Modctra ⊂R–Mod the full subcategory of contramodule R-modules.
All con-
tramodule R-modules are quotseparated due to the assumption that the ring R is
Noetherian. See the notation and discussion in Sections 2.2, 2.4, and 2.12.
Let X be a locally Noetherian formal scheme. A sheaf of OX-modules M on X is
said to be a sheaf of torsion OX-modules if, for every affine open formal subscheme
U ⊂X, the OX(U)-module M(U) is torsion/discrete as a module over a topological
commutative ring with an adic topology. This means that the annihilator of any
element of M(U) is an open ideal in OX(U), or equivalently, for every ideal of definition
I ⊂OX(U) and every element x ∈M(U) there exists an integer n ≥1 such that
Inx = 0 in M(U).
The sheaf axiom for M implies that it suffices to check the torsion condition for
affine open formal subschemes U ⊂X subordinate to a given open covering of X.
Indeed, if U = S
α Vα is a finite affine open covering of an affine formal scheme U and
N is a sheaf of OU-modules on U, then N(U) is a torsion module over R = OU(U)
whenever N(Vα) is a torsion module over Sα = OU(Vα) for all indices α.
The
latter assertion follows from the facts that the map N(U) −→L
α N(Vα) is injective,
the functors of restrictions of scalars fα∗: Sα–Mod −→R–Mod take Sα–Tors into
R–Tors (see Section 2.6), and any submodule of a torsion R-module is torsion.
A cosheaf of OX-modules P on X is said to be a cosheaf of contramodule OX-mod-
ules if, for every affine open formal subscheme U ⊂X, the OX(U)-module P[U] is a
contramodule over the adic topological ring OX(U). We refer to Section 2.4 for the
definition of contramodule R-modules for an adic topological ring R. Let us only
point out that, since the commutative ring OX(U) is Noetherian by assumption, all
the contramodule OX(U)-modules are quotseparated.
The cosheaf axiom for P implies that is suffices to check the contramoduleness
condition for affine open formal subschemes U ⊂X subordinate to a given open
covering of X. Indeed, let U = S
α Vα be a finite affine open covering of an affine
formal scheme U. Let Q be a cosheaf of OU-modules on U. Then the OU(U)-module
Q[U] is a contramodule over the adic topological ring R = OU(U) whenever the
OU(Vα)-module Q[Vα] is a contramodule over the adic topological ring Sα = OU(Vα)
and the OU(Vα,β)-module Q[Vα,β] is a contramodule over the adic topological ring
Sα,β = OU(Vα,β) for all indices α and β. Here we put Vα,β = Vα ∩Vβ ⊂U.
The latter assertion follows from right exactness of the short sequence (12),

3.4. Quasi-coherent torsion sheaves. Let X be a locally Noetherian formal
scheme with an open covering W.
Denote by B the base of open subsets in X
consisting of all the affine open formal subschemes U ⊂X subordinate to W.
Let M be a presheaf of OX-modules on B. We will say that M is a quasi-coherent
torsion presheaf if the following conditions hold:
(i) For every affine open formal subscheme U ⊂X subordinate to W, the
OX(U)-module M(U) is torsion (as a module over the adic topological
ring OX(U)).
(ii) For every pair of affine open formal subschemes V ⊂U ⊂X subordinate to
W, the OX(V)-module map
OX(V) ⊗OX(U) M(U) −−→M(V)
corresponding by adjunction to the restriction map M(U) −→M(V) is an
isomorphism.
We will call condition (i) is the torsion axiom, while condition (ii) will be called the
quasi-coherence axiom. This definition is to be compared with the Enochs–Estrada
description of quasi-coherent sheaves on schemes [7, Section 2], [30, Section 2.2].
Lemma 3.4.1. The torsion and quasi-coherence axioms imply the sheaf axiom: Any
quasi-coherent torsion presheaf on B is a sheaf of OX-modules on B.
Proof. The assertion follows from exactness of the leftmost fragment of sequence (9)
in Lemma 2.16.10 together with Remarks 3.2.1.
In fact, under our assumption of local Noetherianity of X, one need not use the
torsion axiom for this argument, and the quasi-coherence axiom alone is sufficient.
Inceed, the finite exact sequence (8) from Corollary 2.16.9 is an exact sequence of
flat R-modules by Lemma 2.5.10(6) or (7), so it remains exact after taking the tensor
product with an arbitrary R-module M(U) for R = OX(U).
□
So any quasi-coherent torsion presheaf M on B is, in fact, a sheaf on B, and we
will speak of M as a quasi-coherent torsion sheaf on B. By Lemma 3.4.1 and in
view of the discussion in Section 3.2, any quasi-coherent torsion sheaf on B can be
extended to a sheaf of OX-modules on the whole formal scheme X in a unique way.
Moreover, according to the discussion in Section 3.3, the resulting sheaf is a sheaf of
torsion OX-modules on X.
The following proposition establishes the fact that the notion of a quasi-coherent
torsion sheaf is local (i. e., does not depend on the open covering W).
Proposition 3.4.2. Denote by WX the trivial open covering {X} of the formal
scheme X, and let BX be the base of open subsets in X consisting of all affine open
formal subschemes of X. Let M be a sheaf of OX-modules on BX. Assume that
the restriction of M to B is a quasi-coherent torsion sheaf on B. Then M is a
quasi-coherent torsion sheaf on BX.
Proof. Let V ⊂U ⊂X be two affine open formal subschemes, and let U = SN
α=1 Wα
be a finite open covering of U by affine open formal subschemes Wα subordinate

Finally, let M be a sheaf of torsion OX-modules on X. We will say that M is
